Palantir 新簽訂單放緩了？蘋果服務
被 AI 影響了？財報焦點 : Palantir +
Apple 蘋果 | 26 年 10 ⽉
KP@FOMOSOC AND BENITA HSU
NOV 3 ∙ PAID
READ IN APP
這次我們想做個新嘗試。
之前幾期的「財報解剖」⼀向以極致的深度著稱，但⽼實說，並⾮每次財報公
佈，都有必要把所有維度拉出來重新解構⼀遍。
有些時候，最關鍵的變化其實只集中在兩三個核⼼變數上。
因此，我們推出了這個更聚焦、節奏更快的變體版。

雖然篇幅縮減，但我們對商業本質的思考沒有打折。這裡保留了最精華的洞
察，保證依然⽐市⾯上絕⼤多數的財報分析更有⾒解。
這份分析由我們的分析師  Benita 主導撰寫，我負責校正觀點並做最後把關，希
望能⽤更⾼效的⽅式，幫⼤家抓出最值得關注的產業訊號。
Palantir ⼜⼀次完美財報，企業 AI
開始真正變現，但新簽訂單卻放緩了？

### Page 2

Palantir 第三季⼜交出了⼀份近乎沒有瑕疵的財報。
2026 年第三季營收來到  23.13 億美元，年增  96% 、季增  20% ，創下公司史上
最⾼的年增率，同時也是連續第  13 個季度營收成⻑加速。

### Page 3

其中美國商業營收⼤增  148% ，調整後營業利益率從去年同期的  51% 躍升⾄
62% ，推升  Rule of 40 達到  158% 。
同時，公司還把第四季營收指引調⾼到約  26.51 億美元，⾼於市場原本預期的
約  24.7 億美元（⾼出約  7% ）。
只要第四季順利達成指引， Palantir 的年化營收規模就將正式跨過  100 億美元
⼤關。
不過， Palantir 的「好」，已經不是新聞了。
我們會將這次的分析分為 4 個重點。
重點⼀：企業 AI 開始真正變現 —— 從百萬美元試點，⾛向千萬美
元的營運系統
過去這兩年，許多企業在導⼊ AI 時，最常⾒的做法就是去買⼤模型的 API 、做
個內部聊天機器⼈，或是花錢做⼀堆概念驗證（ PoC ）。
但試了⼀圈之後，許多企業開始感到挫折。
⼀⽅⾯，對外串接純⼤模型供應商，很難完全保護公司內部的機密資料與營運
知識；另⼀⽅⾯，讓 AI 寫寫信、做個摘要並不難，真正的挑戰在於如何讓 AI
融⼊公司的⽇常⼯作流程，並創造出⾜以回本的經濟效益。

為了解決這個痛點， Palantir 過去透過實戰⼯作坊（ AIP Bootcamp ，近期已進⼀
步演進為打造 AI 代理的 AgentCamp ），讓企業帶著⾃⼰的真實資料，在短時間
內做出⼀套能實際運作的系統。
到了今年，這套模式更延伸到「主權 AI （ Sovereign AI ）」。
這裡談的 AI 主權，重點在於讓企業⾃⼰掌握資料、業務邏輯與模型選擇權。 企
業可以⾃由更換底層模型，並把真正值錢的營運知識留在⾃⼰的系統裡。
在這個架構下，企業可以利⽤⾃⼰累積的營運資料，在 Palantir 的 Ontology （本
體論）架構下評估並微調開放權重模型，完全不必每件事都去呼叫最昂貴的頂
級⼤模型。
在這次法說會上，有兩個案例特別有說服⼒：
第⼀個是國防科技公司 L3Harris 。

### Page 4

他們利⽤⾃家資料微調開放模型後，在不到 48 ⼩時內，就讓這套主權模型的表
現超越了原本使⽤的前沿⼤模型，⽽且初步結果顯⽰整體成本⼤幅降低了
95% 。
第⼆個案例則是連 NVIDIA ⾃⼰都在⽤。
NVIDIA 透過 Palantir 評估並重新訓練（ post-training ）⾃家的開放模型，結果成
功讓旗下體積最⼩、效率最⾼的模型，在表現上⼤幅超越了⾃家最⼤的模型。
企業在⽇常營運中，往往更需要⼀個真正懂內部業務、且成本合理的專屬
模型，勝過⼀味追求最昂貴卻不懂公司流程的通⽤⼤模型。
舉個簡單的例⼦，⼀家製造商如果要⽤ AI 判斷哪些零件該優先採購，它真正需
要的是模型能讀懂公司的供應商名單、⽣產排程、庫存限制和交貨承諾，並根
據最新狀況隨時調整。
Palantir 想掌握的，正是把底層模型轉化為實際⼯作流程的這⼀層價值。
當企業發現這套做法真的能解決問題，從試點轉為正式⼤單的速度就相當快。

### Page 5

這次財報中， Palantir 公布了四個在⼀兩季內從⼩型試點迅速擴張成⼤型合約的
案例，擴張後的年化合約價值（ ACV ）合計達到 7,650 萬美元：
家電製造商： 從 100 萬美元的初始試點，擴張⾄ 1,500 萬美元的年化合
約。
⼯業材料公司： 從近 300 萬美元試點，擴張⾄ 2,900 萬美元。
全球運動品牌： 從 160 萬美元試點，擴張⾄約 2,100 萬美元。
服飾企業： 透過 AgentCamp 啟動 250 萬美元試點，隨後擴張⾄ 1,150 萬美
元。
其中，那家家電製造商的變化特別有代表性。
他們在第⼆季才剛花 100 萬美元試⽔溫，很快就轉成 300 萬美元的合約；結果
到了第三季，合約⾦額⼜⼀⼝氣放⼤五倍來到 1,500 萬美元，使⽤範圍直接橫
跨銷售（商務）、財務與供應鏈三⼤部⾨。

### Page 6

這代表客戶⼀旦把單⼀功能跑順了，就會開始把不同部⾨的⼯作流程全部串進
同⼀個平台，預算規模也跟著翻倍跳升。
重點⼆：美國商業營收⾸度超⾞政府 —— ⽼客戶越買越多，撐起
第⼆顆成⻑引擎
當單⼀客戶的合約⾦額從⼀百萬變成上千萬美元，反映在全公司的總體財報
上，就促成了這次最具指標性的轉折：
Palantir 的美國商業營收，歷史上第⼀次超越了美國政府營收。
我們可以看看第三季美國市場這兩⼤業務的表現：
美國商業客戶營收：達到  9.84 億美元，年增  148%（季增  29% ）。
美國政府客戶營收：達到  9.53 億美元，年增  96% 。

### Page 7

如果把海外市場也加進來，整體商業總營收來到  11.7 億美元，同樣在歷史上⾸
度超越整體政府營收的  11.5 億美元。
⽐起破紀錄的成⻑率，營收組合的翻轉其實更具代表性。
過去市場對  Palantir 的印象，多半停留在政府、國防與情報系統的承包商；但
隨著商業營收全⾯超⾞，華爾街在它⾝上套了⼗年的「政府承包商」標籤，在
這⼀季正式失效。
過去市場對 Palantir 的印象，多半停留在政府、國防與情報系統的承包商。這類
業務雖然⾨檻極⾼，但容易受到政府預算審查與採購週期的影響。
值得注意的是，這次商業營收能夠超⾞，背後依然伴隨著極為強勁的政府業
務。
美國政府營收年增 96% ，幾乎翻倍；純粹是因為美國企業客戶導⼊的速度衝得
更快，讓公司成功⻑出了第⼆顆規模相當、⽽且爆發⼒更強的成⻑引擎。
如果再往下⼀層看，會有⼀個⽐營收交叉更有意思的發現。

### Page 8

第三季美國商業客戶的總數來到 686 家，年增 29% ；但同期美國商業營收卻⼤
增了 148% 。為什麼營收成⻑的速度，會遠遠甩開客戶數量增加的速度？
答案就在於「淨收⼊留存率（ NDR ）」。這⼀季 Palantir 的整體淨收⼊留存率，
從上⼀季的 157% 進⼀步攀升到 165% 。
這說明了推動營收翻倍的主⼒，很⼤部分來⾃既有客戶持續加碼。
就像前⾯提到的製造商⼀樣，⼀家企業可能先⽤ Palantir 管庫存，覺得好⽤之
後，接著把採購、排程、財務到 AI 決策全部放進來。
不過，這波企業 AI 變現潮⽬前還是⾼度集中在美國。

### Page 9

相較於美國商業營收 148% 的⾼成⻑，第三季國際商業營收只有 1.84 億美元，
年增 21% ，季增更只有 1% 。
這意味著美國企業對軟體轉型的接受度依然領先全球，⽽未來能否把美國驗證
成功的模式複製到海外市場，將是後續觀察的重點。
重點三：營收翻倍但員⼯數幾乎沒變 —— ⾼營業槓桿背後的結構
與代價
把前⾯兩件事串起來看：當客戶透過⼯作坊體驗產品後迅速擴⼤採⽤，⽽且導
⼊之後⼜會⾃⼰越買越多（ NDR 165% ），這會對 Palantir 的成本結構帶來什麼
影響？
執⾏⻑ Alex Karp 在接受媒體（ MarketWatch ）訪問時特別提到了⼀個營運細
節：
Palantir 在美國商業營收⼤增 148% 的同時，全公司的員⼯總數（ Headcount ）
幾乎維持持平。

### Page 10

在傳統的企業軟體產業裡，公司如果想把營收規模擴⼤⼀倍，通常得按⽐例招
募⼤量的業務代表、售前顧問與⽀援⼯程師，⼈⼒成本也會跟著⽔漲船⾼。
但 Karp 形容 Palantir 的成⻑基本上不太依賴傳統意義上的業務員。對他們來說，
產品本⾝（ Foundry 、 Ontology 加上 AIP ）在⼯作坊裡的實際表現，就是最好的
銷售⼯具。
更重要的是，因為底層的資料整合與基礎架構在初期導⼊時就已經建好了，當
客戶後續決定把合約從 300 萬美元擴⼤到 1,500 萬美元時， Palantir 並不需要再投
⼊同等⽐例的⼯程⼈⼒從頭開發。
當營收以接近翻倍的速度成⻑，交付這些服務所需的總⼈數卻沒有等⽐例
增加，這展現了軟體業相當強勁的營業槓桿。
這項特質解釋了為什麼 Palantir 能在⾼成⻑的同時，交出亮眼的獲利數字：
第三季 GAAP 營業利益率達到 47% ，調整後（ Non-GAAP ）營業利益率⾼達
62% ，進⽽推升 Rule of 40 來到 158% 的⽔準。
不過，這種⾼營業槓桿背後也有相對應的代價。

雖然公司的總員⼯數幾乎持平，但為了留住頂尖⼈才並獎勵績效，第三季的股
權激勵費⽤（ SBC ）從去年同期的 1.72 億美元⼤幅增加到 3.19 億美元，幾乎翻
了⼀倍。
這也正是  GAAP 營業利益率（ 47% ）與調整後營業利益率（ 62% ）之間相差 15
個百分點的主要原因。
重點四：拆解 TCV 放緩與 RPO 持平的真相

### Page 11

這份完美財報裡唯⼀的裂縫，在於反映未來幾年營收可⾒度的單季「新簽總合
約價值（ TCV ）」與「未履約義務（ RPO ）」出現了明顯放緩：
整體  TCV ：第三季為  29.18 億美元，年增率僅  6% （相⽐第⼆季為年增
49% ），⽽且較上⼀季的  33.73 億美元下滑了  13.5% 。
美國商業  TCV ：第三季為  14.63 億美元，年增率降⾄  11% （相⽐第⼆季為
年增  153% ），為  2023 年以來的最低單季增速。
剩餘交易價值（ RDV ）與未履約義務（ RPO ）：單季來看，整體  RDV 季
增僅  2% （美國商業  RDV 季增  4% ，低於上⼀季的  27% ）， RPO 則幾乎持
平。
當季營收⼤增  96% 、新簽訂單卻只成⻑  6%，這兩個數字在財務邏輯上不可能
⻑期並存。
當季營收主要是由既有客戶擴張所推動，⽽新簽約指標的放緩，⾃然讓部分投
資⼈開始擔⼼未來幾年的營收可⾒度。
針對這個現象，財務⻑ David Glazer 與經營團隊在法說會上的解釋是：

### Page 12

許多客戶為了快速導⼊ Sovereign AI 的初期功能、急著讓系統趕快上線，所以
在第三季簽下了好幾筆千萬美元級別、但「合約期限⽐往常更短」的訂單。
管理層同時強調，「 Sovereign AI ⾰命才剛滿⼀季」，並預期隨著客戶使⽤深
⼊，這些合約的年限與規模都會隨時間增加。
管理層的這番解釋，其實也剛好從會計規則上說明了為什麼 RPO 幾乎持平：因
為 Palantir 的 RPO 定義，本來就不計⼊初始期限少於 12 個⽉的短期合約。
如果第三季新簽的  Sovereign AI ⼤單多半屬於⼀年以內的短約，那麼不會反映
在 RPO 上、同時壓低單季 TCV 總額，確實符合財務邏輯。
此外，如果我們把時間拉⻑來看，⻑約存量指標其實沒有單季數字看起來那麼
疲弱：
整體  RDV （剩餘交易價值）： 年增率仍達  55% 。
美國商業  RDV ： 達到  65.2 億美元，年增率⾼達  79% 。
過去  12 個⽉（ TTM ）美國商業  TCV ： 累計達  61.14 億美元，年增

62% 。
換句話說，單季簽約指標放緩，還不等於企業端的需求轉弱。真正的問題在於
估值給出的容錯空間極⼩。
以約  178 美元的股價與  24.03 億股計算， Palantir 的市值約  4,286 億美元；扣除
帳上  104 億美元的現⾦與美國公債後，企業價值（ EV ）約為  4,182 億美元。
對照全年營收指引中值  85.32 億美元，企業價值倍數（ EV/Sales ）⾼達  49 倍；
就算⽤第四季指引年化的  106 億美元來算，也⾼達約  39 倍。
將近  40 到  50 倍的營收定價，隱含市場要求這家公司在跨過百億美元年化營收
之後，未來⼗年還得維持約  50% 以上的年複合成⻑率。
在這麼⾼的⾨檻下，市場沒有太多耐⼼等待。
下⼀季投資⼈最想驗證的，就是這批短約能不能如管理層所說，順利續約成更
⻑、更⼤的多年期合約。
從更⼤的產業趨勢來看，企業 AI 的投資重⼼正在轉移。
第⼀波浪潮，⼤家搶著買 GPU 、買算⼒、買⼤模型 API ；但進⼊第⼆階段，企
業真正關⼼的是如何結合內部資料與⼯作流程，讓 AI 實際改善營運並省下成

本。
Palantir 已經在美國市場證明⾃⼰能吃到這波轉型的紅利。

### Page 13

接下來決定它⻑期價值的關鍵，就在於這批新導⼊的短期合約能否在未來幾季
轉化為⻑約存量，並將這套商業模式進⼀步複製到更廣⼤的海外市場。
蘋果：營收創紀錄、⽑利被記憶體成本
壓縮， Service 令⼈失望？
Apple 這次財報，表⾯上相當不錯。
第四季營收來到 1,145 億美元，年增 12% ；每股盈餘 2.10 美元，年增 14% 。主
⼒產品 iPhone 營收成⻑ 17% ， Mac 更是⼤幅成⻑ 24% 。

### Page 14

但在這些亮眼數字背後，如果仔細拆解財報細節與管理層在法說會上的說法，
會發現幾個值得留意的變化。
⾸先是⽑利率。
這⼀季表⾯上 47.9% 的⽑利率，其實包含了約 1 個百分點的關稅退款⼀次性收
益（每股盈餘 2.10 美元裡，也有 0.05 美元來⾃同⼀筆退款）。

### Page 15

扣掉這筆⼀次性收益後，本業⽑利率⼤約是 46.9% ；但就算⽤這個基準來⽐，
Apple 給出的下⼀季（ 12 ⽉季度）⽑利率指引落在 45% ⾄ 46% ，依然在往下⾛。
要知道， 12 ⽉季度是 Apple 傳統的銷售最旺季，營收規模放⼤通常會推升⽑利
率，為什麼這次反⽽逆勢下滑？
財務⻑ Kevan Parekh 在法說會上給出了答案：下⼀季⽑利率下滑，「主要」反映
記憶體成本上升，「另外也包括 Siri AI 上線的部分成本」。
這句話其實就是貫穿整份財報的核⼼線索：全球 AI 浪潮正同時從「硬體成
本」與「軟體商業模式」兩端夾擊 Apple 。
AI 確實帶來了新的成⻑機會，但也正在改變 Apple 原本最賺錢的獲利結構。
我們可以把這次財報拆解成五個環環相扣的重點來看：
重點⼀：硬體的成本逆⾵ —— 全球 AI 算⼒戰，為什麼由 Apple
先買單？

### Page 16

下⼀季⽑利率下滑的最⼤推⼿，來⾃記憶體成本的飆升。 這背後反映了⼀個很
現實的產業現象： Apple 雖然想靠 AI 賺錢，但現在卻得先為全球的 AI 資料中⼼
基建買單。
CEO John Ternus 在法說會上直接⽤「前所未⾒（ unprecedented ）」來形容當
前的記憶體價格環境。
其實在 9 ⽉這⼀季， Apple 就已經感受到記憶體漲價的壓⼒，只是當時⼿裡還有
早先囤積的低價庫存，加上其他⾮記憶體零組件成本下降，才抵銷掉部分衝
擊。
但隨著低價庫存逐漸消耗，這股成本壓⼒將在 12 ⽉季度正式浮現；管理層也坦
⾔，在 12 ⽉季度之後，記憶體採購價格預計仍會持續上升，⽽研調機構更普遍
預期這波供應緊俏會⼀路延續到 2027 年。
為了應對這場結構性的缺貨， Apple 已經採取了兩個動作：
直接漲價： 6 ⽉底先調漲了 Mac 與 iPad 的價格， 9 ⽉⼜將 iPhone 售價調⾼
約 100 美元，且管理層並未排除未來進⼀步漲價的可能。

### Page 17

砸錢鎖定產能： Apple 的庫存⾦額單季⼤增 31% 、年增⾼達 154% ，來到約
145 億美元；從 6 ⽉到 9 ⽉季度，製造採購承諾⾦額更是⼀⼝氣暴增了 330
億美元。
憑藉龐⼤的現⾦流與規模， Apple 搶貨的能⼒絕對⽐中⼩型 Android ⼿機廠或 PC
廠強得多。
但記憶體終究是每⼀台硬體裝置中無法妥協的核⼼成本，當漲價轉嫁給消費者
到了⼀定極限、開始有拉⻑換機週期的⾵險時，硬體⽑利率勢必得承受壓⼒。
全球掀起 AI 資料中⼼擴建潮，最先賺到錢的是記憶體⼤廠，⽽包括 Apple
在內的終端硬體商，正在為這場硬體通膨買單。
重點⼆：⾼⽑利新武器卡關 ——iPhone Duo 的產能與初期品管
挑戰

### Page 18

既然記憶體漲價壓縮了硬體⽑利，最⾃然的解法，就是多賣⼀點⾼單價、⾼⽑
利的新產品來拉⾼平均售價（ ASP ）。
這就帶出了市場最關注的新品： Apple ⾸款折疊機 iPhone Duo 。
iPhone Duo 在 9 ⽉底財報結算後才正式上市，因此第四季財報還沒有反映它的
營收。
從需求⾯來看，這款新機⾮常成功，預購迅速秒殺，出貨時間⼀路排到明年⼀
⽉下旬。
但問題出在供給⾯。
這次法說會上， CEO John Ternus 特別安排營運⻑ Sabih Khan ⼀起上線回答供應
鏈問題。
Khan 坦⾔， iPhone Duo ⽬前處於「產能受限」的狀態，且「很難預測供需何
時能達到平衡」。

### Page 19

這種罕⾒不給時間表的說法，意味著折疊螢幕與轉軸的量產爬坡難度，⽐過去
傳統 iPhone ⾼出許多。
更⿇煩的是，初期有極少數⽤戶反映內螢幕轉軸處出現摺痕或裂紋的品質問
題；雖然 Apple 已經在協助換機，但緊繃的產能也讓部分地區的換貨等待時間
拉⻑。
除了折疊機，過去受限於先進製程（隨著產品線轉向 2 奈⽶已逐漸緩解）的
Mac 與 iPad ，到了 12 ⽉季度依然⾯臨缺貨，⽽這次卡住產能的瓶頸，剛好⼜是
前⾯提到的記憶體不⾜。
換句話說，儘管下⼀季營收預計仍能成⻑ 7% ⾄ 10% 、有望創下公司歷史新
⾼，但記憶體漲價加上⾼階新品缺貨，讓硬體業務在短期內很難幫公司撐起⽑
利率。
這時候， Apple 就更需要仰賴⾼⽑利的 Services （服務業務）來穩住獲利基本
盤。
第四季 Services 營收只占公司總營收約 28% ，卻因為⾼達 76% 的⽑利率，貢獻
了全公司將近 44% 的⽑利。

### Page 20

硬體負責把⼈帶進⽣態系， Services 負責賺取⾼利潤，這是 Apple 最核⼼的賺錢
公式。
然⽽，正如財務⻑在⽑利率指引中透露的，連 Services 這座⾼⽑利堡壘，也從
下⼀季開始要⾯對 AI 帶來的成本考驗。
重點三： Siri AI 終於有了收費⽅向，但會不會稀釋 76% 的⾼⽑
利？
Apple 在今年 WWDC 就已經宣布，部分需要雲端模型處理的 AI 功能將設有使
⽤額度，使⽤者未來可以付費取得更多運算容量。
這次法說會上， Goldman Sachs 分析師 Michael Ng 進⼀步追問： iCloud+ 是否正
在轉型成 Apple AI 商業化的重要⼯具？
管理層給出了肯定的答案，確認未來需要更多伺服器端 AI 運算能⼒的使⽤者，
可以付費購買額外容量。
過去⼤家訂閱 iCloud+ ，買的是「儲存空間」，⽤來放照⽚、影⽚和備份檔案。
到了 AI 時代，使⽤者額外付費購買的變成了「運算能⼒」，像是讓 Siri 分析複
雜⽂件、執⾏多次雲端請求，或是呼叫⼤型模型處理⼯作。

### Page 21

坐擁超過 25 億台活躍裝置、超過 15 億個付費訂閱的 Apple ，要推這項新服務當
然很有優勢。
假設未來 Apple 能吸引 5,000 萬⼈、每⽉額外付 10 美元使⽤進階 AI 功能，⼀年
就能帶來 60 億美元的營收，相當於⽬前全年 Services 營收的 4.9% 左右。
但從財務⾓度來看，這⾨⽣意跟過去的數位服務有⼀個本質上的差異。
傳統數位服務的營收增加，不太需要同⽐例增加成本；但⼤型 AI 模型每次執⾏
雲端推論，背後都在消耗真實的算⼒資源。使⽤者越頻繁使⽤複雜的 AI 功能，
Apple 要承擔的伺服器與推論成本就越⾼。

### Page 22

過去每收到 100 美元服務收⼊， Apple 可以留下 76 美元⽑利；但到了 AI 服務
時代，營收增加的同時，背後往往伴隨著持續攀升的雲端推論成本。
這也解釋了為什麼財務⻑ Kevan Parekh 特別強調， Siri AI 的雲端推論費⽤將
計⼊ Services 的銷貨成本，⽽且相關成本已經開始反映在 12 ⽉季度的⽑利率指
引中。
同時， Apple 還透露了⼀個會計處理上的改變：當 Siri AI 成為硬體裝置隨附的服
務時，公司會將⼀部分的硬體收⼊分配給這項服務，並在裝置使⽤期間逐步認
列。
這代表就算消費者沒有額外掏錢加購算⼒， Apple 的 Services 營收與成本結構，
也會因為 AI 功能的推出⽽重新洗牌。
因此，接下來觀察 Apple 的 AI 變現能⼒，除了看有多少⼈願意付費，更要看每
增加⼀美元的 AI 收⼊，究竟能留下多少⽑利。
這也是為什麼 Apple 持續投⼊⾃研晶⽚與裝置端 AI ，除了讓⼿機跑得更順，
背後更有明確的財務考量：把越多的運算留在裝置端處理，⻑期下來就能

省下越多的雲端推論成本。

### Page 23

重點四：華爾街真正擔⼼的事 —— 當 AI Agent 成為⼊⼝，
Apple 還能收多少過路費？
如果說推論成本影響的是「利潤率」，那 Morgan Stanley 和 Evercore 分析師在
法說會上追問的，則是關係到「營收來源」的根本問題：
當 AI 從聊天機器⼈演變成能替使⽤者執⾏任務的 Agent （代理），會不會威脅
到 Apple 現有的 Services 業務？
我們可以想像⼀個很⽇常的場景。
現在如果你想規劃⼀趟旅⾏，通常會先打開 Google 搜尋機票，進到航空公司網
站⽐價，接著打開訂房 App 找飯店，最後分別完成付款。整個過程必須切換好
幾個不同的平台與 App 。
但在不久的將來，你可能只需要對⼿機裡的 AI Agent 說⼀句：「幫我安排下週末
去東京的旅⾏，機票加飯店預算三萬台幣，飯店希望離⾞站近⼀點。」
接下來， AI Agent 就會⾃動幫你搜尋、篩選，並直接串接不同服務完成預訂。
從頭到尾，你可能連訂房 App 都沒有打開過。
這個轉變最核⼼的影響，在於「誰掌握了決定權」。 過去是使⽤者決定要打開

哪個 App ；未來則是 AI Agent 決定要調⽤哪⼀家服務商。

### Page 24

⼀旦消費者習慣把⽇常決策交給 ChatGPT 、 Gemini 或其他第三⽅ AI Agent ，
Apple 過去靠 iOS 與 App Store 築起的收費關卡就會⾯臨兩個挑戰：
App Store 分潤壓⼒： 如果第三⽅  Agent 直接透過網⾴、外部  API 或其他
⽀付管道完成交易， Apple 對數位商品與訂閱交易的抽成能⼒就會被繞
過。
搜尋授權收⼊重分配： Apple 每年從搜尋引擎拿到的巨額授權⾦，建⽴在
「 iPhone 是最重要搜尋⼊⼝」的商業價值上。當  AI Agent 逐漸取代傳統
搜尋與  App 操作，這個⼊⼝的經濟價值勢必會重新洗牌。

### Page 25

⾯對這個問題， John Ternus 的回答展現了 Apple 的防守策略。
他強調， iPhone 是使⽤者最重要的個⼈資訊中⼼，裡⾯存放著聯絡⼈、訊息、
照⽚、⾏事曆、健康與財務資料。
AI 如果想真正理解使⽤者需求並跨 App 完成任務，就必須取得這些個⼈脈絡與
系統權限。
這讓未來的 AI Agent 競爭，分成了兩種不同的控制權：
需求⼊⼝： 誰最懂使⽤者想做什麼，誰來決定推薦哪些商品與服務（這
是  OpenAI 、 Google 正在搶的）。
執⾏⼊⼝： 誰能安全地讀取個⼈資料、呼叫  App 功能，並完成最後的操
作（這是  Apple 透過  iOS 、 App Intents 與  Siri AI 牢牢握在⼿裡的）。

### Page 26

Apple 很清楚⾃⼰未必要做出全世界最龐⼤的模型，但它必須利⽤作業系統與
隱私權限的優勢，把「需求」與「執⾏」整合在⾃⼰的平台上，確保使⽤者依
然透過 Siri 來完成⽇常任務。
⽽這場⼊⼝保衛戰之所以迫在眉睫，從這次 Services 的財測就能看出端倪。
Apple 第四季 Services 營收達到創紀錄的 315.7 億美元，年增約 10% ；但下⼀季
的成⻑指引卻放緩到 8% ⾄ 9% 。
管理層解釋，放緩的主因來⾃「第三⽅授權收⼊」與「匯率」變化，其次則是
部分 App Store 與⼿遊業務的調整。

### Page 27

值得注意的是，管理層⼝中的「第三⽅授權收⼊」，背後很⼤⼀部分就是
Google ⽀付給 Apple 的搜尋引擎分潤。
雖然單季度的波動未必完全來⾃ AI Agent 的衝擊，但這正好印證了華爾街的擔
憂：只要搜尋授權與 App Store 這兩⼤⽀柱稍微放緩， Services 的成⻑動能就會
⽴刻感受到壓⼒。
重點五：現在的股價，到底在賭什麼？
把前⾯硬體與軟體的拼圖全部放在⼀起，我們最後來看估值。
以財報當⽇收盤價約 338 美元、以及 FY2026 實際每股盈餘 8.98 美元計算，
Apple 的歷史本益⽐約為 37.7 倍（財報公布後盤後股價回落⾄約 329 美元，換算
也⾼達 36.6 倍）。

### Page 28

37.7 倍的本益⽐聽起來很貴，但「貴」是⼀個很模糊的詞。換個問法會清楚很
多：如果今天⽤ 338 美元買進 Apple ，然後⼀直抱著不賣，你每年⼤概能賺多
少？
有⼀個很簡單的算法：
⻑期年報酬  ≈ 現⾦殖利率  + ⻑期成⻑率
現⾦殖利率，就是公司⼀年賺進的⾃由現⾦流，除以你付出的市值。
Apple 現在市值接近 5 兆美元，⼀年的⾃由現⾦流⼤約只有市值的 2.6% 。
換句話說，⽤ 338 美元買進，第⼀年真正拿回的現⾦回報只有 2.6% 左右，剩下
的報酬全靠未來成⻑。
⽤這個邏輯反推，可以從兩個⾓度看現在的股價：
如果假設  Apple ⻑期每年成⻑  3% ：
338 美元的股價，換算下來的⻑期年報酬⼤約是  6.1% （註：若以純現⾦殖利率
2.6% 直接相加則約為  5.6% ）。

### Page 29

同⼀時間，美國⼗年期公債殖利率約  4.9% ，⽽且幾乎沒有⾵險。
這代表買  Apple ⽐買公債多承擔了股價波動的⾵險，每年卻只多換來約  1 個百
分點左右的⾵險補償；⽽在歷史上，美股投資⼈要求的⾵險補償通常落在  4%
到  5% 。
如果投資⼈要求每年  7% 的報酬：
這只⽐公債多  2 個百分點左右，已經算很客氣了。
但要做到這樣， Apple 必須永遠每年成⻑約  3.6% 以上。
⼀般估值模型給成熟公司的永續成⻑率，多半抓在  2% 到  3% ；要求  3.6% ，等
於要求⼀家市值近  5 兆美元的巨頭，永遠跟著整個美國經濟的速度⼀起⻑⼤。
這兩個⾓度講的是同⼀件事：現在的股價，幾乎沒有留空間給意外。
這時候再回頭看⽑利率，就能理解市場為什麼對那 1 到 2 個百分點的變化這麼
敏感。

### Page 30

以 2026 財年全年 Services ⾼達 1,233 億美元的營收規模來算，服務⽑利率只要
每下降 1 個百分點，⼀年就少掉約 12.3 億美元的⽑利。
若放⼤到全公司約 4,790 億美元的總營收來看，全公司⽑利率每少 1 個百分點，
扣稅後⼤約會少掉每股 0.27 美元的盈餘；⽤ 37.7 倍的本益⽐換算，等於股價直
接少了約 10 美元。
更別忘了，連計算 37.7 倍本益⽐所⽤的 8.98 美元 EPS 起點，裡⾯都還包含了⼀
筆關稅退款的⼀次性收益。
所以這次財報最值得思考的地⽅，在於現在的股價，其實已經先預設了 Apple
會有個完美結局：
市場預設記憶體漲價很快會過去、 Siri AI 的雲端算⼒成本控制得住，⽽且第三
⽅ AI Agent 也搶不⾛ iPhone 的流量⼊⼝。
對 Apple 來說，投⼊ AI 同時是⼀項成⻑投資，也是⼀筆為了保護每年 1,233 億
美元服務⽣意的必要⽀出。
只要硬體缺料漲價、 AI 推論成本攀升、或是⼊⼝分流這三件事當中有任何⼀件

拖得⽐預期更久，市場除了要下修獲利預估，連那 37.7 倍的估值倍數本⾝都可
能⾯臨重新定價。
⽽在現在這個價位，後者帶來的影響往往更⼤。

### Page 2

An update on AI and US equities

Both stocks and investor views relating to the AI trade have been exceptionally 
volatile during the last few months. Most of the volatility has been concentrated in the 
AI infrastructure complex, but the latest earnings season also drove sharp moves in the 
hyperscalers and among software stocks. 
Below we address three of the most common AI-related questions we have received 
from clients in recent weeks: 
1. What is the outlook for AI capex? 
2. What revenues are required to generate a sufficient return on recent AI 
investments? 
3. What outlook for AI is the equity market pricing?

1. What is the outlook for AI capex? 
The largest US hyperscalers are on track to spend $800 billion in capex in 2026 and 
consensus estimates imply they will spend $1.1 trillion in 2027. Consensus 2027 
capex estimates have been revised higher by $175 billion since the start of the Q2 
earnings season. Commentary from the hyperscalers during earnings season described a 
supply-constrained environment and large revenue backlogs, and these factors are 
driving the companies to tap both debt and equity markets to support additional 
investment spending. Based on consensus estimates, capex in 2027 is on track to reach a

Our baseline forecast is that hyperscaler capex spending will increase substantially 
in 2027 but that the rate of both capex growth and upside surprises to consensus 
estimates will diminish relative to recent quarters. GS equity analysts estimate that 
hyperscaler capex will register $1.2 trillion in 2027 and $1.4 trillion in 2028. Their 
estimates are $130 billion and $100 billion above the consensus estimates, respectively, 
but nonetheless imply a deceleration in capex growth from nearly 100% in 2026 to 54% 
in 2027 and 12% in 2028.

The need for external financing and potential supply bottlenecks will likely 
constrain the rate of capex growth next year. Hyperscaler capex spending now 
exceeds cash flow from operations, which means large further growth will increasingly 
need funding via debt and equity issuance. While hyperscalers have added leverage, 
their balance sheets do not appear to be a binding constraint and their interest coverage 
ratios remain extremely healthy. Our credit strategists instead point to concentration in 
the investment grade market as the more relevant bottleneck, which will require 
additional funding from private credit and equity issuance. Potential government 
restrictions on data center construction or an intentional slowdown in frontier model 
roll-out could also act as potential constraints on spending, as could shortages of labor, 
power, memory, or other inputs. Constraints on capex represent a risk to the near-term 
earnings of AI infrastructure beneficiaries, but could help ease investor concerns about 
overbuilding.

### Page 4

Going forward, token demand growth will need to outpace declining token prices to 
support continued growth in investment spending. Frontier models are currently a 
key source of demand for hyperscaler compute. However, the rise of competitive 
open-source models has contributed to a decline in average token prices. Measures of 
frontier token demand slowed in July, simultaneous with sell-offs of many AI 
infrastructure stocks, but accelerated again in August. 
For most AI infrastructure companies, decelerating AI investment spending 
suggests decelerating EPS growth. AI infrastructure companies, and semis in 
particular, have been the clearest beneficiaries of the capex surge. Continued upward 
revisions to consensus 2027 capex estimates should support continued positive 
revisions to consensus 2027 EPS estimates for many AI infrastructure stocks, but the 
magnitude of revisions will likely diminish relative to what occurred in H1 2026.

### Page 5

2. What revenues are required to generate a return on AI investments? 
Our equity analysts estimate that the hyperscalers would need to generate annual 
AI revenues of roughly $300 billion in the next few years to break even on their 
average annual capex investments in 2026 and 2027. These estimates reflect their 
expectations for average annual AI capex in 2026 and 2027 for the US hyperscalers, 
including AMZN, GOOGL, META, MSFT, ORCL, and SPCX. The breakeven revenues 
required for this capex represent the sum of annual depreciation and operating 
expenses. Exhibit 8 provides an estimated sensitivity of required AI revenues the 
hyperscalers must generate in different return on invested capital (“ROIC”) scenarios. 
These required revenue estimates are anchored on average annual capex in 2026 and 
2027 and would argue for even larger estimates based on cumulative capex.

### Page 6

The hyperscalers’ current estimated AI revenues remain below the amount that 
would allow them to break even on their capex, but those revenues are growing 
quickly and revenue backlogs are sizable. The hyperscalers have started to monetize 
their AI investments. Year/year cloud revenue growth for AMZN, GOOGL, MSFT, and 
ORCL has accelerated from 25% in 2024 to 48% in Q2 2026. In dollar terms, annualized 
cloud revenues in excess of their 2024 growth rate registered nearly $70 billion in Q2 
and have been roughly doubling during the past few quarters. The three major US public 
cloud companies (AMZN, GOOGL, MSFT) have also announced a combined revenue 
backlog of $1.7 trillion, which provides line of sight into these companies’ ability to meet 
required revenue targets.

### Page 7

The magnitude of capex in 2026 and 2027 points to even larger “required” annual 
revenues in the next few years for the “application layer” of companies buying 
much of that compute from the hyperscalers. These revenues reflect the additional 
costs and profit margins of application companies on top of the “required” hyperscaler 
revenues selling compute. EBIT margins for large-cap software companies have 
averaged 40% during the past five years. We utilize EBIT margins rather than gross 
margins to include the costs of both training and inference, and assume additional 
expenses alongside compute. The range of potential required revenues is wide 
depending on which companies constitute the application layer and how their costs are 
structured.  
Exhibit 11 provides a range of potential “required” revenues based on hyperscaler 
ROIC and application layer EBIT margins. For example, the application layer would 
have to generate over $1 trillion in annual revenues if those companies had a 30% EBIT 
margin and the hyperscalers generated a 10%-20% return on invested capital. 
The “required” application layer revenues that would justify current capex are large 
but potentially achievable. For example, estimates of global advertising and software 
spending each equal roughly $1.5 trillion in 2026. Our economists estimate that total 
annual AI US labor productivity gains that will potentially accrue to capital total roughly 
$1.8 trillion. However, not all of these gains will necessarily accrue to the infrastructure 
and application layer companies. In addition, another year of strong capex growth in 
2028 would raise the hurdle further. AI-related revenues and capex needs would be 
substantially larger accounting for physical AI or the potential emergence of AGI.

### Page 8

3. What outlook for AI is the equity market pricing? 
While market participants express skepticism about the durability of AI 
infrastructure stocks’ earnings growth, that view is at least partially priced. The 
median AI infrastructure stock has de-rated from 32x consensus forward earnings in 
April 2026 to 22x today. In addition to tracking standard P/E multiples, comparing 
recent changes in market cap to recent changes in earnings can signal how long the 
market expects current earnings strength to persist. Among various AI infrastructure 
baskets, that ratio remains most elevated among optical networking, electrical grid, and 
liquid cooling stocks while expectations appear most conservative among 
semiconductors and utilities. 
Semiconductor valuations appear to embed particularly skeptical views regarding 
the sustainability of current earnings strength. Valuations have declined sharply 
among Semis stocks, which is typical among cyclical companies with strong near-term 
growth but uncertain medium-term growth. We believe the investment opportunity is 
more compelling in fabless chip stocks than in memory stocks. Our equity analysts are 
Buy-rated on NVDA and AVGO but Neutral-rated on MU.

### Page 9

Memory stock prices imply earnings will decline in coming years, but the degree of 
pessimism does not appear particularly extreme relative to past cycles. Memory 
stocks currently trade at a 2-year forward P/E of 4x, half their 15-year average, but 
Memory firms currently enjoy gross margins of roughly 80%, more than double their 
historical average. To bring the current P/E of memory stocks to its 15-year average, 
earnings in coming years would need to decline by roughly 50%. While this decline is 
large in absolute terms, memory is a cyclical industry and has experienced larger EPS 
declines, including outright negative earnings, during previous cycles. This pricing will 
prove too pessimistic if 2028 forecasts are realized, but many investors have expressed 
skepticism about rotating back into higher beta pockets of the infrastructure trade as 
capex growth begins to normalize.

### Page 10

In contrast, the fabless chip stocks also trade at discounted valuations but have had 
less extreme margin expansion. Margins for the largest fabless chip stocks are only 
modestly above their historical average. Earnings in the next few years would need to 
decline by about 10% to bring the P/E back to its historical average. This is similar to the 
magnitude of historical realized cyclical earnings declines for the group.

The likelihood of decelerating capex growth should be a positive catalyst for the 
hyperscalers. We previously laid out three conditions for the hyperscalers to regain 
leadership. These include decelerating macroeconomic growth, demonstrated 
monetization of AI investment, and decelerating capex growth. Our economists believe 
US economic growth is no longer accelerating. The hyperscalers have already started to 
demonstrate signs of monetization, and we expect capex growth will slow in 2027. 
Uncertainty about the length, magnitude, and return on investment for this capex cycle 
will likely persist. However, the hyperscalers have de-rated to their lowest P/E multiples 
in over a decade, and the group now trades just four turns above the median S&P 500 
company, its smallest premium on record. While investors have recently been focused 
more on the hyperscalers’ negative free cash flows than on their earnings, visibility into 
improving FCF as revenues accelerate and capex decelerates should be supportive for 
the stocks.

### Page 11

We expect the progression of the AI trade towards application roll-out will remain a 
source of return dispersion. We believe the “winners” and “losers” from the application 
layer will take several quarters to play out, as AI applications get built and adoption 
broadens. 
The threat of disruption had weighed on the share prices of many Software stocks 
earlier this year, but the group has rebounded sharply in recent weeks. Reported 
Software fundamentals have shown little sign of AI disruption, with annual recurring 
revenue growth for the median stock in GS analyst coverage accelerating slightly in 
recent quarters. A group of Software stocks has rallied by 45% and is now within 8% of 
its high reached in late 2025. Software stocks focused on IT infrastructure and 
cybersecurity have posted the strongest returns, while enterprise management software 
remain below their previous highs.

### Page 12

Consumer-facing businesses with high switching costs have also recently emerged 
as a potential area of disruption. The increasing use of agents, including by new 
consumer-facing tools such as Muse, may disrupt the business models of some 
consumer firms. Our colleagues in Global Banking & Markets developed a basket of 
“consumer inertia” stocks (GSXUSWCH) where user stickiness is driven more by effort 
required to take action rather than product differentiation. The basket includes 
insurance, telecom, travel, subscriptions, marketplaces, and fintech. The basket has 
lagged a sector-neutral hedge by 5 pp since mid-September.

### Page 13

Enterprise AI adoption remains in early stages but spending has recently 
accelerated, suggesting the impact of AI on corporate earnings should grow 
increasingly visible in coming quarters. The Census Bureau survey suggests that 40% 
of large US firms are using AI in the production of goods and services. Our analysis of Q2 
earnings commentary suggests roughly 50% of S&P 500 companies are using AI in the 
context of productivity, but very few companies have quantified the productivity uplift 
today. However, the revenue run rates of model providers and alternative measures of 
company spending on AI have accelerated during the last several months alongside the 
increasing use of agentic harnesses. 
In contrast with past periods of market exuberance, the equity market today does 
not appear to be pricing expectations of widespread productivity gains. Baskets of 
AI productivity beneficiaries have traded roughly sideways on a relative basis YTD, 
matching the trajectory of near-term forward EPS estimates. Similarly, the measure of 
market-implied long-term growth expectations in Exhibit 22 is only slightly above the 
historical average (for details, see US Equity Views: The next phases of the AI trade).

我們深度分析  Marvell 併購  Lumentum 將產⽣的綜效，雙⽅投資⼈
為何都不看好，未來 5 年財務衝擊及 outside the box thinking
ANDREW LU ON GLOBAL SEMI/TECHS
NOV 2 ∙ PAID
READ IN APP

What’s new?
Marvell Technology and Lumentum on Nov 2, 2026 announced that
they have entered into a deﬁnitive agreement for Marvell to acquire
Lumentum in an all-stock transaction. Under the terms of the
agreement, Lumentum stockholders will receive a ﬁxed exchange
ratio of 4.5 shares of Marvell common stock for each share of
Lumentum common stock they own. The transaction has an implied
enterprise value of $115 billion for Lumentum, representing
approximately $1,132 per share of Lumentum common stock and a

premium of approximately 23.9% based on each company’s
unaﬀected closing share price as of October 28, 2026, the last
trading day before market speculation regarding a potential
transaction began. Post-closing, current Marvell stockholders will
own approximately 67% of the combined company on a fully diluted
basis, while Lumentum stockholders will own approximately 33%.
但⾃從併購消息提前傳出來， Marvell 股價連續重挫，公司於 11 ⽉ 2 ⽇  收盤
171.41 美元，⽽  Lumentum 每股股東只能拿到 771.345 美元，這⽐起

Lumentum 收盤價 684.4 美元，溢價⼤幅縮⽔到 12.7% ，也⽐上週討論到的
併購溢價⼤幅縮⽔了 32% ，明顯表⽰ Marvell 及  Lumentum 的投資⼈都不看

### Page 2

好這次併購，到底是綜效不⾜，價格太⾼，未來 5 年財務預測衝擊⼤，還是擔
⼼  Marvell 買  Lumentum 在 AI 基礎建設投資週期頂點？
The synergies
Marvell 的核⼼意圖在於從單純的晶⽚設計商，升級為  AI 資料中⼼光電互連
（ Optical Interconnect ）的垂直整合巨擘：
綜效⼀：取得  InP 雷射製造能⼒，控制  CPO 的關鍵供應瓶頸
這可能是本次併購最有戰略價值的⼀部分。當  AI 叢集算⼒從  800G 邁向
1.6T 、 3.2T 甚⾄到  CPO （共同封裝光學元件）時代，⾼速傳輸的功耗與延遲
瓶頸已轉移⾄光電轉換。 Marvell 握有頂尖的  PAM4 DSP 、 TIA/Driver 及
ASIC 交換晶⽚，但⽋缺關鍵的光源硬體。 Lumentum 則掌握化合物半導體
（ InP 磷化銦⾃有晶圓廠）、連續波（ CW ）⾼功率雷射與  OCS （光電路交換）
技術。合併後能實現從數位、類⽐到光⼦端的全鏈路協同設計（ Co-

design ）。
對  CPO ⽽⾔， ASIC 及交換晶⽚只是其中⼀部分。完整⽅案仍需要：
⾼速電訊號處理與光電轉換。
CW Laser 或外部雷射光源（ ELS ）。
光調變器、接收器與光引擎。
⾼密度封裝、光纖耦合及散熱設計。
付費訂閱戶可分享我們對於四⼤綜效分析，雙⽅投資⼈
為何都不看好，在擔⼼什麼？對 Marvell 2027-2031
年的財務預測衝擊及 outside the box thinking
Subscribed
Marvell 買下  Lumentum ，相當於將原本必須外購的關鍵光源能⼒納⼊同⼀
家公司。這有機會改善設計整合、可靠度、功耗及量產交期，也降低受到外部

### Page 3

雷射供應商產能限制的⾵險。但應注意， CPO 不⼀定要求雷射與交換  ASIC 封
裝在同⼀顆晶⽚上；外置雷射仍然是重要的架構選項。
Source: Andrew Lu on global semis/techs
綜效⼆：從銷售  DSP ，升級到銷售完整  Optical Engine
以傳統  800G/1.6T 光收發器為例， Marvell 主要提供電⼦端  DSP 與相關類
⽐晶⽚，⽽  Lumentum 提供光源、光學器件及部分收發器解決⽅案。合併
後， Marvell 可以直接與客戶共同設計光電互連系統，⽽不僅是提供分離式零
件。這個差異的財務意義，是提⾼每條光連結的可取得價值（ Revenue
Content per Optical Link ），⽽⾮只靠光連結出貨量增⻑。尤其在未來  3.2T
光模組、 NPO 與  CPO 世代，系統設計越緊密，晶⽚、光引擎與雷射之間的聯
合驗證價值就越⾼。不過這並不保證客戶會購買整套⽅案。⼤型雲端業者可能
仍採取多供應商策略，並⾃⾏主導系統設計。

綜效三：正⾯挑戰  Broadcom 的  AI 互連⽣態系
過去  Marvell 與  Broadcom 的競爭，主要集中在  Custom ASIC 、 SerDes 、
DSP 、交換晶⽚及網路互連。併⼊  Lumentum 後，競爭範圍將延伸⾄雷射、
光引擎與  OCS ，這場競爭的核⼼不再只是「誰的  DSP 晶⽚⽐較便宜」，⽽是
誰能⽤較低的  pJ/bit 功耗與更可靠的光學系統，協助客戶建造更⼤規模的  AI
Cluster 。然⽽， Broadcom 仍有交換晶⽚市占、客戶關係與成熟網路軟硬體
⽣態系優勢。收購  Lumentum 不代表  Marvell ⽴即追平  Broadcom 的市場
地位。

### Page 4

Source: Andrew Lu on global semis/techs
綜效四：將雷射供應保障轉變成  Custom ASIC 的競爭籌碼
假設  Google 、 Amazon 或其他  Hyperscaler 未來發出新的  Custom AI
ASIC ，其評估標準可能不再只是算⼒、晶⽚價格與  TSMC 產能，⽽是整套  AI
Rack 的：
1. XPU 運算能⼒。
2. Scale-up / Scale-out 頻寬。
3. 光連結功耗與訊號完整性。
4. 雷射供應保障。
5. 整機櫃部署成本及可靠性。
Marvell 如果能同時提供  ASIC 設計與有保障的光學供應鏈，就可能提⾼其在
客戶系統架構設計中的參與度。
Why both Marvell and Lumentum investors worry?
1. 33% 的股權稀釋
全股票收購⼤幅膨脹股本： 交易完成後， Lumentum 原股東將持有合併
公司約  33% 股權， Marvell 既有股東僅剩約  67% 。

EPS 稀釋陷阱： 根據股權結構倒推， Marvell 現有業務每賺  100 美
元， Lumentum 加上淨綜效必須貢獻⾄少約  49 美元的獲利，才能勉強
打平原有股東的每股盈餘（ EPS ）。若  Lumentum 獲利未達爆發性預
期，既有股東權益將被嚴重攤薄。
2. 在週期⾼點買在「天價估值」
Lumentum 股價在收購前已因  AI 光模組需求⼤漲，交易估值隱含⾼達  35 倍
以上的遠期本益⽐。市場質疑  Marvell 是在  AI 基礎設施週期的估值頂峰，溢
價收購⼀家具有⾼度資本⽀出（ CapEx ）與重資產製造屬性的光學元件廠。
Fabless IC 設計公司併⼊化合物半導體晶圓廠，後續擴產將持續吞噬⾃由現⾦
流（ FCF ）。 Lumentum 的  AI 光通訊業務雖有結構性需求，但仍與超⼤型雲

### Page 5

端客戶  CapEx 、光模組需求及光學庫存循環⾼度相關。合併並不必然提⾼收
⼊來源的分散程度，反⽽⼤幅增加對  AI 基建景氣循環的曝險。
3. 漫⻑的交割期（ 2028 上半年）與反壟斷⾵險
官⽅預計交易要到  2028 年上半年才能完成，橫跨近兩年時間。
中國監管變數： 光學元件與通訊雷射⾼度牽動全球雲端與網通供應鏈，
Lumentum 在中國有廣泛業務與供應鏈佈局，這項超⼤型交易極可能遭
遇中國反壟斷機構（ SAMR ）的漫⻑審查與嚴苛附加條件。兩年內若  AI
資本⽀出週期降溫， Marvell 將承受巨⼤的合約與套牢⾵險。
4. 客戶中⽴性⽡解（ Supplier Neutrality ）與競業威脅
晶⽚客戶轉單⾵險： 原本向  Marvell 採購  DSP 晶⽚的光模組廠商（例
如旭創、新易盛等），或使⽤  Lumentum 雷射的網通巨頭（如  Cisco 、
Broadcom 陣營⽣態），可能因  Marvell 跨⼊整機 / 光模組⾃產⾃銷⽽產
⽣排擠與防備，甚⾄引發客戶轉向中⽴競爭對⼿（如  Coherent ）。

商業合作  vs. 資本併購： 雙⽅原本在  2026 年初就已透過商業聯盟共同
展⽰  OCS 技術，投資⼈質疑為何不能以商業代⼯或授權合作，⽽必須付
出千億美元代價冒險合體。
Financial impacts
我們根據彭博社分析師的預測， Lumentum 不管在營收，⽑利，還是
GAAP/non-GAAP 營業利潤及調整後 EPS 的 5 年（ 2026-2031 ）複合增⻑率
皆明顯優於 Marvell ， GAAP/non-GAAP 的營業利潤率也優於  Marvell ，加上
管理階層給出了每年超過  10 億美元的  Run-rate 成本綜效⽬標，並預期在併
購完成後⾸年（ Year 1 post-close ）即可實現  Non-GAAP EPS 的增厚
（ Accretive ）。但  Lumentum 在營收，⽑利，營業利潤的合併後貢獻⽐例皆
明顯不⾜股權稀釋⽐例的 33% ，這讓 Marvell 合併後的  adj EPS 低於併購前
市場預期 adj EPS 達 5-10 個百分點。外加超過 10 倍股東權益淨值的龐⼤溢價

將逐年攤提， Marvell 合併  Lumentum 之後的 GAAP EPS 也將明顯低於之前
預期。

### Page 6

Source: Andrew Lu on global semis/techs, Bloomberg
Outside the box thinking
第⼀， Marvell 正從  Fabless 模式 , 轉變成帶有重要製造資產的半導體集團
⻑期以來， Marvell 的商業模式主要仰賴外部先進晶圓代⼯及封裝⽣態系。
Lumentum 則有⾃有  InP 及其他化合物半導體製造能⼒。這表⽰合併後公司
將同時擁有⾼階  ASIC 設計與特殊半導體製造資產。好處是能控制稀缺製程產
能；壞處是必須承擔設備投資、產能利⽤率與製造良率⾵險。市場可能必須重
新評估  Marvell 是否仍適合享有純⾼成⻑  Fabless ASIC 公司的估值倍數。
第⼆， CPO 將使  Optical Connectivity 的商業模式變得更接近客製化
ASIC

### Page 7

傳統光模組由多家零件商提供標準化產品；未來部分⾼階  CPO 光引擎可能需
要與交換  ASIC 、 XPU 以及系統架構⾼度共同設計。如果這個⽅向成⽴，競爭
將逐漸從單純⽐價格，轉向：
誰掌握系統架構設計。
誰擁有完整  IP 與可靠度驗證能⼒。
誰能提供有保障的量產產能。
誰能在功耗、頻寬與單位成本之間取得最佳組合。
Marvell 可能希望將過去與  Hyperscaler 合作  Custom ASIC 的經驗，移植
到  Optical Connectivity 業務。但我們認為應區分兩個市場：傳統
Pluggable Optics 仍可能保持多供應商與模組化結構，⽽⾼度客製化  CPO
的整合優勢才可能真正擴⼤。
第三，這筆併購也可能是  Marvell 對光通訊獲利分配的⼀種防禦
當  1.6T 、 3.2T 及  CPO 時代來臨，單⼀光連結的成本與價值分配可能改變。
假如  DSP 成本占⽐下降，⽽雷射、光引擎、先進封裝與光學耦合占⽐提升，
原本⾼度依賴  DSP 的公司即使維持市占率，也不⼀定能取得最⼤經濟價值。

economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.39 / 26 October 2026EDITORIAL
WHY CHINA RESISTS REBALANCING ITS GROWTH MODEL?
China has never exported as much as it does now, and its trade surplus has never been higher. At the same time, its
economic growth is slowing, held back by weak domestic demand. The imbalances in China’s growth model, and the ex-
ternal imbalances they give rise to, are therefore continuing to worsen. With Europe taking a tougher line and the United

States, with which China shares nothing more than ‘strategic stability’, the standoff looks set to continue. However, the
European Union and China would have a great deal to lose from a lasting deterioration in their relationship. It is in the
interests of both parties to seek to restore the balance. To this end, it is important to understand Beijing’s objectives and
the constraints facing China. Why does the country resist adjusting its growth model? Whilst exports are a vital asset for

sustaining economic activity and asserting China’s technological and industrial power, this strategy also comes at a cost.
IMBALANCES ARE WORSENING
A K-shaped economy. China’s economic growth slowed to 4.6% year-
on-year (y/y) over the first three quarters of 2026, from 5.0% in 2025.
More worryingly, the gap is widening between weak domestic demand
and the very strong performance of exports, driven by artificial intelli-
gence equipment and green technologies. Furthermore, the dynamism

of export-oriented industries has not, so far, had any positive knock-on
effects on other sectors or on households.
Domestic demand is struggling. The property crisis, now in its sixth
year, and a sluggish labour market are weighing on household confi -
dence and consumption. Since the start of 2026, households have been
prioritising debt reduction (bank loans to households have fallen by
1% y/y), house sales have continued to fall, and retail sales have been

stagnating (Chart 1). On the investment front, the decline is becoming
more pronounced and widespread: it is affecting not only the property
sector but also infrastructure and many manufacturing sectors (with
the exception of the textile industry, transport and communications,
and electronic and electrical equipment).
Exports remain very buoyant. Exports continue to be the main driver of
growth (their net contribution is expected to account for around a third

of real GDP growth in 2026, as it did in 2024–2025). Against a backdrop
of rebound in global trade, Chinese companies maintained their market
share at 14.6% of total exports (measured in US dollars) in the first
half of 2026, as they had in 2025. Growth in goods exports accelerated
(21.9% y/y in USD over the first nine months, compared with 5.2% in
2025), driven by both volumes and prices. These have rebounded after
three years of decline, driven by rising costs of raw materials and the

prices of goods required for AI, but they have increased at a slower rate
than the global price average. Exports from high-tech sectors (batte -
ries, electric vehicles, robots and AI equipment) are posting the highest
growth rates (+55.8% y/y for all AI-related goods over the first nine
months of 2026).
Record external surpluses. Imports, which had been stagnating since
2022, have been recovering since the start of the year (up 27.2% y/y
over the first nine months), also driven by both volumes and prices.

The countries benefiting from this rebound in the Chinese market are
commodity exporters and Asian producers integrated into AI value
chains (South Korea in particular). Otherwise, China has significantly
reduced its reliance on imports over the past few years. Overall, the
trade surplus has grown at a slower rate in 2026 than in previous years.
Nevertheless, it has still reached a record high: USD1,249 billion over
the twelve months to September 2026, which is three times its 2019

level (see Chart 2).

### Page 2

4
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.39 / 26 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
Meanwhile, the current account surplus is expected to approach a re -
cord level of USD 800 billion in 2026, representing nearly 4% of China’s
GDP (a ratio already seen in the late 2000s). As a proportion of global
GDP , this is unprecedented (at 0.6%).
Imbalances set to persist. The imbalances inherent in the growth mo-
del are likely to persist, fuelled notably by the strong price and non-

price competitiveness of Chinese goods. This competitiveness remains
underpinned by: i/ innovation efforts and the move up the value chain
in high-tech sectors and the low-carbon transition; ii/ low production
costs (labour, low-cost credit, land and energy, tax benefits 1); iii/ the
yuan’s exchange rate. Its slow appreciation since mid-2025 has not
offset the cumulative depreciation from 2022 to 2025 (nominal and
real depreciation due to low inflation), and its real effective exchange

rate (REER) remains 10% below its 2021 average. At the end of 2025,
the IMF estimated that the REER was undervalued by between 12%
and 20%.
Beijing’s economic strategy is exacerbating imbalances. The 15th Five-
Year Plan2, launched this year, prioritises innovation, high-tech (ex -
port-oriented) sectors and technological self-reliance above all else,
whilst reforms to the social security system and private consumption
remain on the back burner (see Chart 3). Furthermore, fiscal policy,

which had provided moderate support for household spending for a
time in 2025, was tightened in 2026 (until the recent announcements
of new measures for the final quarter).
WHY BEIJING IS DIGGING ITS HEELS IN
Beijing is not giving ground on any key issues. Faced with trading
partners calling on China to rebalance its economic model, reduce in -
dustrial subsidies and strengthen the yuan, Beijing responds that the
trade surplus reflects the competitiveness of its goods, points to the

United States’ insufficient savings, and uses critical materials as a bar-
gaining chip in negotiations.
The need for growth. Exports are a key driver of growth at a time when
the Chinese economy is undergoing a period of transition. The country
has moved away from its pre-2022 model, which was based on in -
vestment in property and traditional industries and financed by debt.
Growth is now driven by ‘new productive forces’, and exports must pro-

vide producers with the market that domestic demand does not. Fur -
thermore, innovation and the roll-out of AI must stimulate productivity
gains and offset the impact of the shrinking labour force on economic
growth. It is essential to limit the slowdown of growth in order to sup-
port job creation and absorb the economy’s excess debt.
The strategic argument. Exports are a means of asserting China’s in -
dustrial and technological supremacy, weakening its competitors, and

strengthening ‘national security’. They place China at the heart of va -
lue chains in the most strategic industries, such as AI and the low-car-
bon transition. Furthermore, the pursuit of self-sufficiency and import
substitution — which exacerbate external imbalances — also help to
build national sovereignty by reducing dependencies.
A political and fiscal choice. Expanding the social safety net is seen as
a solution for providing long-term support for household consumption3

by reducing their savings rate (estimated at 35.7% of disposable income
in 2025, which is more than double the average rate for the euro area).
1 According to the IMF, the cost of China’s industrial policy amounts to about 4% of GDP . See IMF, ‘Industrial Policy in China: Quantification and Impact on Misalloca-
tion’, August 2025: https://doi.org/10.5089/9798229017855.001
2 See BNP Paribas, EcoWeek, China’s 15th Five-Year Plan: prioritising power over addressing imbalances

3 The IMF estimates, for example, that doubling social expenditure (health, education, social services and basic pensions) in rural areas could reduce savings and
raise the ratio of private consumption to GDP by 1.8 pp in the medium term, at an annual fiscal cost of 1% of GDP . See IMF, People’s Republic of China 2025 Article IV,
February 2026: https://doi.org/10.5089/9798229038911.002
However, this is not a priority for Beijing, certainly due to ‘ideologi -

cal’ mistrust of the welfare state and a desire for fiscal prudence.
A major reform of the healthcare or pension systems would place a
strain on public finances in both the short and long term, particularly
as the population ages. Whilst the central government remains in a
sound financial position, local governments are already under strain
following several years of falling land sales proceeds, high deficits and
rising debt. The authorities do not have a large fiscal room for ma -

noeuvre and wish to preserve it in anticipation of future shocks.
LIMITATIONS AND POSSIBLE ADJUSTMENTS
In China, a society gripped by anxiety… Economic imbalances are ta -
king their toll on households. The emphasis placed on manufacturing
rather than services is having a negative impact on employment and
wages (the share of value added allocated to employees’ pay is esti -
mated at 50% in the services sector and 41% in manufacturing). The
downturn in the property market is eroding households’ wealth, and

their savings are mainly held in low-yield bank accounts. Finally, social
spending is rising slowly (by 5.2% a year since 2020, compared with
9.7% over the previous five years).
… and enterprises under pressure. The low prices of Chinese goods,
both for export and on the domestic market — resulting from excess
production capacity and ‘involution’ — are eroding corporate profit
margins. Total profits for industrial enterprises have recovered this
year but they are highly concentrated, particularly in AI-related sec -

tors, while the proportion of industrial companies reporting losses has
continued to rise (30%, compared with 26% in 2022). These vulnerabili-
ties are having increasing contagion effects on the rest of the economy,
notably through worsening payment delays and rising pressure on the
profitability of financial institutions.

for a changing
world
Abroad, trading partners are becoming increasingly intolerant. This
is particularly the case of the United States and the European Union 4,
which have absorbed 24% and 28% respectively of China’s trade surplus
over the last twelve months (data from China’s General Administration
of Customs). Tensions between the US and China could rise again in
2027 when the current truce (which has mainly led to a reduction in

tariffs following the peak in H1 2025) comes to an end. Against a back-
drop of competition for technological supremacy and industrial sove -
reignty, the trend towards decoupling between China and the US could
continue, thereby reducing market opportunities for Chinese producers.
Europe, for its part, is reinforcing its strategic approach and expanding
its toolkit of measures, moving towards more targeted restrictions and
stricter market access conditions, especially in case of serious market

distortions). Rather than pursuing a strategy of decoupling, the EU is
seeking to reduce risks and provide greater protection for critical sec -
tors (including the automotive and chemical industries) without clo -
sing its market and whilst continuing to attract Chinese investment.
This strategy will also lead to tensions. With imbalances becoming
increasingly unsustainable, what course of action would make it pos -
sible to avoid a sudden adjustment precipitated by episodes of crises?

4 See BNP Paribas, EcoWeek, When it comes to China, Europe should focus less on its trade deficits and more on its investments
A ‘concerted’ adjustment is difficult to envisage. A negotiated adjust -
ment, along the lines of the 1985 Plaza agreements, seems unlikely in
the current international and military context. Furthermore, China and
its trading partners largely disagree on the diagnosis and the causes of
the imbalances. Moreover, a simple revaluation of the yuan would, on

its own, have only a limited effect on China’s competitiveness.
A gradual adjustment may be possible… in the long term. However, the
reduction of imbalances will need to come both from partner countries
(through increased savings) and from China. In the short term, China
can ease trade tensions by investing more abroad, which is all the
easier given that it has huge current account surpluses. This would
enable it to maintain its access to foreign markets and support the

production capacity of its trading partners. More structural changes
(a more flexible exchange rate regime, less fiscal support for indus -
tries and more support for households) will only be feasible once Bei -
jing considers that the costs of its export-led model will outweigh the
strategic gains. A key challenge for trading partners will be to work out
how to shift this cost-benefit balance.

Document 467: Why hasn’t there been a major financial crisis in recent years_ (2026-11-03)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Why hasn’t there been a major financial crisis in recent years_ (2026-11-03).pdf
Report source: DB Research
Date: 2026-11-03
Pages: 19
Status: ok

### Page 1

Deutsche Bank 
Research Institute

Why hasn’t there been a major 
financial crisis in recent years?

1 
Authors 
Henry Allen 
Macro Strategist

Peter Sidorov, CFA 
Senior Economist

Camilla Siazon 
Research Analyst

November 3, 2026 
— The economist Hyman Minsky warned, in essence, that “stability is destabilising” . He 
argued that a lengthy period of financial stability could be problematic, as it induced 
risk-taking and complacency amongst investors, laying the foundation for the next 
crisis. For example, lenders relax their terms and borrowers are more willing to extend 
themselves, as nobody wants to miss out. This dynamic makes economies inherently 
prone to financial panics and boom-bust cycles. 
— But since the 2008 crisis, and the subsequent sovereign crisis in the Eurozone, we 
have yet to see another financial crisis happen on a scale that had a lasting market 
and macroeconomic impact. There have been clear moments of concern, as in early 
2023 with the regional bank turmoil, but nothing that has caused a lasting impact. So 
this led us to ask why. 
— In this paper, we examine why the post-GFC era has seen fewer financial crises. 
Explanations include the reforms made after 2008 to avoid a repeat, the lower 
proportion of financial assets held by the banking system, as well as the speedier 
reaction of policymakers when wobbles have occurred. We then consider a few 
candidates that could cause the next crisis, including AI vulnerabilities, private credit, 
and stretched fiscal positions. 
— Ultimately, financial crises haven’t been abolished, but the reforms of recent years 
have made it harder for shocks to propagate through the traditional banking system. 
Moreover, policymakers have become far more inclined to intervene when initial signs 
of stress have emerged.

### Page 2

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 2 Deutsche Bank AG
Section 1: What is a financial crisis, and what causes them?
There isn’t a single technical definition of a “financial crisis”, and they come in many
different types. Commonly, we think of banking crises like 2008, but they can also
encompass currency/balance of payments crises, debt crises and other forms too.
Given currency and sovereign crises have been more common in emerging markets
(the UK's ERM crisis in 1992 and the euro crisis being the two most notable DM

occurrences), historical analysis in developed economies has tended to focus
heavily on banking crises. Non-bank funding or market-liquidity crises are often not
categorised separately in the literature, but they have become more common in
recent years. This includes the "dash-for-cash" stress at the onset of the Covid
pandemic in March 2020 and the 2022 LDI crisis in the UK.
But for all the different types of financial crisis, there are common themes between

them. Often, they are accompanied by asset bubbles that burst in a non-linear
fashion. We generally see a severe tightening in credit conditions, which in turn
flows through into a broader economic contraction. Frequently, there are
contagion-like dynamics that can be difficult to stop. And in the modern world, they
usually require policymakers to step in, stabilising the situation with interventions.
At risk of oversimplification, we note four factors that determine the emergence and

the severity of a financial crisis. The first three are well-established in the financial
literature, sometimes pooled together into a build-up and a crisis phase
(Brunnermeier & Oehmke 2013), while the fourth has become increasingly
important over the past 15-20 years:
1.  Underlying imbalances. Bubbles and financial imbalances build during
the run-up phase, leaving accumulated risks that materialise when the
crisis erupts. This is the build-up typically described by the early phases of

Kindleberger's model of financial crises.
2.  Trigger. This is the shock that turns an existing vulnerability into clear
financial stress. These are often very difficult to predict and we mostly leave
these outside the focus of this report.
3.  Amplification and financial system fragility. Various factors determine
whether a shock propagates through the financial system, and the
intensity of that shock. These include leverage, liquidity and maturity

mismatches, interconnectedness, and the concentration of exposures.
Other channels in the financial system can then amplify the shock,
including bank runs, fire sales and margin calls, and negative net worth
effects, sovereign–bank loops etc.
4.  Intervention/policy response. This has been increasingly important in
recent decades as the size and scope of policy response to crises has
increased. The outright size can vary, and intervention can occur across

several dimensions, ranging from direct intervention in financial markets,
to counter-cyclical macroeconomic policy that mitigates the impact on the
real economy.
Clearly, these factors are not independent and the interaction between them is what
determines the severity of a financial crisis.

### Page 3

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 3
One important aspect is the joint role that underlying imbalances and amplification
play. While theoretical models such as Diamond & Dybvig highlight the potential for
a bank run that triggers a broad crisis, empirical research shows that in practice, it
is exceedingly rare for a bank run to lead to bank failure in the absence of an
underlying solvency issue (Correia, Luck and Verner 2026). In other words, the

presence of amplification channels alone does not lead to a crisis.
Conversely, in the absence of amplification, it is possible to get significant
turbulence in asset prices, but without a financial crisis. For instance, a bear market,
wider credit spreads, or even a recession do not in and of themselves imply there
is a financial crisis. This was seen clearly with the bursting of the dot com bubble,
a severe equity decline but not a financial crisis, though it did cause a recession. At

the same time, in the chart below, we can see several US equity bear markets that
took place outside of recessions. On other occasions, however, a “Minsky
Moment” can then happen, where even a seemingly minor event can trigger a high-
profile incident that cascades across the financial system.
Figure 1: S&P 500 drawdowns over time, with shaded recessions showing select episodes when the index fell over 20%
without coinciding with a recession
Source :Bloomberg Finance LP, NBER, Deutsche Bank Research

Looking back through history, we can see how variation in these factors has led to
different regimes over time in terms of frequency/severity of financial factors.

### Page 4

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 4 Deutsche Bank AG
Section 2. Why have financial crises been more or less likely
in certain periods of history?
1. Pre-WWII: More frequent financial instability, albeit under a very
different system.
Financial instability was a recurrent feature of the pre-WWII era. During the 19th
and early 20th centuries, there were frequent panics that often led to major slumps.
For instance, the Panic of 1873 sparked a period often known as the “Long

Depression”, which extended into the 1890s for many countries.
In part, financial panics occurred more frequently because policy operated under
very different settings. For instance, the Gold Standard as the monetary system
constrained a countercyclical policy response and transmitted deflation
internationally, while falling prices increased real debt burdens. Moreover, the
Federal Reserve wasn’t established until 1913, and the lack of an effective lender of

last resort meant there was a private intervention during the US Panic of 1907. The
first wave of globalisation also brought new vulnerabilities. Between 1870 and
1913, many emerging economies accumulated debts denominated in gold or
foreign currency, leaving them exposed when capital inflows reversed or exchange
rates weakened.
The biggest and most famous crisis occurred in 1929, when the Wall Street Crash
of that year sparked the Great Depression. The length and depth of that crisis helped

to stimulate the study of modern macroeconomics, with huge efforts to prevent a
repeat over the years ahead. Indeed, the failures of the response to the Great
Depression played a large role in the much swifter response to the 2008 financial
crisis, helping to avoid a second Great Depression.
That experience established much of the modern financial safety net that exists to
this day. For instance in the US, the Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation was

established in 1933. The US and many other countries came off the Gold Standard
as well in this period. There were also regulatory reforms, and the Glass-Steagall Act
separated out commercial from investment banking.
2. 1944-1971: The Bretton Woods era coincided with fewer financial
crises
Around the end of WWII, economic policymakers sought to create an international
monetary system for the post-war era. The relative financial calm that followed

reflected a combination of restrictions on international capital movement, tightly
regulated domestic financial systems, and a system of fixed exchange rates that
aimed to provide stability and prevent the competitive devaluations that occurred
in the 1930s. This saw exchange rates pegged against the US Dollar, which in turn
was convertible to gold at $35 per ounce.
At the same time, capital controls helped preserve monetary policy flexibility, while

maintaining the system of fixed exchange rates. These controls had several
downsides but they also insulated countries from “sudden stop” crises that can
occur, when capital rapidly flees a country. Moreover, capital controls prevented
the rapid buildup of cross-border systemic leverage. So it was more difficult to
generate a global financial crisis in the same way.

### Page 5

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 5
Figure 2: Gold price (USD/oz, log scale)
10
100
1000
1920 1940 1960 1980 2000 2020
In the Bretton Woods
era, the dollar was tied
to gold
Source : Finaeon, Deutsche Bank
Alongside capital controls, there were also domestic reforms in the aftermath of the
Great Depression, which sought to avoid a repeat. As mentioned earlier, the US
Glass-Steagall Act of 1933 separated commercial banking from investment

banking. Another example of the era’s regulated financial system in the US was
Regulation Q, which imposed maximum interest rates that commercial banks
could pay on deposits. That meant depositors could not search for yield and cause
a run on deposits as easily. Another rationale was that with institutions not
competing for deposits, it would avoid a compression in the margin between the
rates banks paid on their lending and borrowing, which would disincentivise a push

into riskier investments.
Finally, economic growth was also stronger in this period, with the period between
WWII and the 1973 oil shock often described as the “Golden Age”. This played a role
in avoiding financial crises too, as faster growth helped keep debt default rates
down, avoiding the kind of “trigger” events above that might spark a financial crisis.
Coupled with financial repression, strong growth also helped to bring down the
high post-war public debt burdens.

Over time however, the peg between the dollar and gold found itself under growing
strain. The economist Robert Triffin identified the “Triffin dilemma”. Supplying the
world with dollar reserves resulted in outstanding dollar liabilities around the world
that exceeded the gold reserves in the US, weakening confidence that dollars could
ultimately be redeemed for gold. Conversely, restricting dollar supply to protect
those reserves and preserve convertibility risked depriving the system of the

liquidity needed to support trade and growth.
These tensions intensified in the late 1960s amid expansionary US policies,
including spending on the Vietnam War and Great Society programmes. The
continued drain in US gold reserves eventually led the US to suspend the
convertibility of the US Dollar into gold, effectively ending the Bretton Woods
system. Initially, there were attempts to bring some kind of fixed-exchange rate
system back, but the world of fiat currencies (with no link to a precious metal)

persists to this day.

### Page 6

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 6 Deutsche Bank AG
3. 1971-2008: Financial crises become more frequent after the
breakdown of Bretton Woods to the Global Financial Crisis
After the breakdown of the Bretton Woods system, financial crises became a more
regular occurrence. While the end of the gold convertibility did not by itself trigger
future crises, it opened the door for wider financial liberalisation, with the resulting
rise in capital mobility and looser domestic financial regulation then helping to

facilitate a global build-up of imbalances.
The 1980s and 1990s were a period of frequent crisis events. The transition away
from capital controls meant that capital could flow more freely across borders.
However, this could also cause a “sudden stop” if investors suddenly pulled their
capital out of a country, contributing to bank runs and currency collapses.
Moreover, many emerging markets borrowed in foreign currencies like the US
Dollar, meaning that a depreciation in their own currency would see this USD-

denominated debt surge in local currency terms. This meant it was a particular
problem if US interest rates were increasing, as it became harder for many
emerging markets to repay their debts. This was a contributing factor behind the
Latin American debt crisis in the early 1980s. And the rate hikes also had domestic
ramifications, being a contributing factor behind the Savings and Loan crisis in the
United States.
With capital flowing more freely, that also played a role in the 1994 Mexican Peso

Crisis, and the 1997 Asian Financial Crisis. A sudden reversal in capital flows led to
crisis and ultimately recession in many countries. The following year, the Russian
financial crisis then took place, and fears grew in the United States about the wider
ramifications, as the hedge fund Long-Term Capital Management (LTCM) came
under pressure. Indeed, this period saw 75bps of pre-emptive rate cuts from the
Federal Reserve.
This volatility also saw the Fed proactively seek to step in and smooth out the

economic volatility. This was broadly successful, but in the process also helped
engender complacency and a further buildup of leverage. Indeed, after the dot com
bubble burst, the federal funds rate was cut to just 1% in mid-2003, where it
remained until mid-2004.
This period saw clear optimism that another Great Depression was highly unlikely.
For instance, Nobel Prize-winning economist Robert Lucas argued in his 2003 AEA
Presidential Address that “macroeconomics in this original sense has succeeded:

Its central problem of depression prevention has been solved, for all practical
purposes, and has in fact been solved for many decades.”
However, as we now know, the 2008 financial crisis occurred, driving the biggest
global downturn since the Great Depression of the 1930s. It turned out that Minsky
had been correct, and a lengthy period without a crisis had once again helped to
induce complacency, laying the foundations for a repeat.

### Page 7

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 7

Section 3: The Post-2008 world: Why have we not had
another big financial crisis?
Since the Global Financial Crisis of 2008, we have thankfully avoided a major
financial crisis. Despite clear moments of stress, most notably around the collapse
of SVB in March 2023, we have not seen a similar wave of bank collapses on that
scale. There are several components to this.
First, post-GFC deleveraging cleared out risks in the system, with countries and
sectors that had excess indebtedness before the GFC experiencing pronounced

declines in leverage.
Second, there have been reforms explicitly designed to avoid a repeat of 2008.
After the crisis happened, there was a clear recognition that gaps in regulation had
exacerbated the crisis, leading to effects that cascaded across the financial system.
For instance, the Basel III reforms required banks to hold more equity, with a
minimum requirement for Common Equity Tier 1 (CET1) capital. They also
introduced the liquidity coverage ratio, which requires banks to hold enough high-

quality liquid assets to ensure they can cover their net cash outflows under a 30-day
stressed scenario. In addition, the net stable funding ratio was brought in, requiring
banks to have sufficient stable funding that exceeded the required amount of stable
funding for a one-year stressed scenario.
At the national level, further reforms were also introduced. In the US, the Dodd-
Frank Act was introduced, with measures including the Volcker Rule that restricted

banks from engaging in proprietary trading, and required the Federal Reserve to
perform an annual stress test of large institutions. Meanwhile in the European
Union, which also experienced a sovereign debt crisis, reforms included a “Single
Supervisory Mechanism”, whereby banking supervision was brought under the
ECB, ensuring harmonised regulatory standards.

### Page 8

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 8 Deutsche Bank AG
Third, the share of financial assets held by banks has fallen since the 2008 crisis.
This hasn’t eliminated risk, but commercial banks are funded by retail deposits that
can quickly fly. By contrast, non-bank financial institutions (NBFIs), e.g. hedge
funds, are not as prone to these bank run dynamics. For instance, a private credit
closed-ended fund is not one where investors can simply redeem their funds on

demand.
We can see this shift in the data. Looking at the FSB’s group of 21 plus the Eurozone,
we can see that the share of assets held by banks has fallen from a peak of 46% in
2008, to 38% in 2024. By contrast, the share held by NBFIs has risen from 43% to
51%.
Figure 5: Share of Assets of Financial Corporations (FSB 21 + EA Group)
35%
40%
45%
50%
2002 2006 2010 2014 2018 2022
Banks Nonbank Financial Intermediation Sector (NBFI)
Source :Haver Analytics, Deutsche Bank

Fourth, the lessons of 2008 have meant that when the financial system has had
wobbles since, policymakers have acted with incredible speed to stop the
turmoil.
This was most obvious when the Covid-19 pandemic began. Whilst a pandemic
was very different to a financial crisis, there was initially huge turmoil in financial
markets. Indeed, the S&P 500 fell by more than a third in just over a month, a decline
at a speed and scale unseen since the Great Depression. Meanwhile, US HY credit

spreads soared to levels last seen around the financial crisis, and at the height of the
turmoil, there was a huge spike in real yields, despite the economy entering a
recession.
Against this backdrop, central banks wasted no time in acting. For instance, the Fed
delivered an emergency 50bp rate cut, followed by another 100bp rate cut.
Moreover, they swiftly resumed Quantitative Easing, and announced the
establishment of multiple new facilities to ensure the smooth functioning of credit

markets. These included a Primary Market Corporate Credit Facility, to support new
bond and loan issuance, along with a Secondary Market Corporate Credit facility,
which offered liquidity for outstanding corporate bonds. Meanwhile, a third facility,
called the Term Asset-Backed Securities Loan Facility, was set up to enable the
issuance of asset-backed securities. Similarly in Europe, the ECB set up a new
Pandemic Emergency Purchase Programme (PEPP), which ultimately had a total

envelope of €1.85tn.
Whilst the pandemic caused a huge economic shock, the swift action meant it did
not translate into a financial one, and the brief tightening in credit conditions was

### Page 9

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 9
unwound over the coming months.
A similar pattern was seen during the regional banking turmoil of March 2023. This
was a financial panic, which initially began with the collapse of Silicon Valley Bank.
However, this began to spread, and Signature Bank also failed. Then in Europe,
Credit Suisse came under investor scrutiny and saw large deposit outflows, which
culminated in a purchase by UBS that included guarantees from the Swiss

government. This coincided with huge market moves, with the 2yr Treasury yield
posting its biggest daily decline in over 40 years, with a 61bps decline on March 13.
However, policymakers swiftly moved to stop any contagion. The US Treasury,
Federal Reserve and the FDIC released a joint statement stating that all deposits at
SVB would be protected, and that the Federal Reserve would provide additional
funding to ensure banks could meet the needs of depositors. Meanwhile, the Fed’s

balance sheet briefly expanded again, despite the fact it had begun the process of
quantitative tightening. These actions ultimately meant the crisis proved contained
and did not lead to a wave of further bank collapses in the manner of 2008.
Figure 6: Federal Reserve Total Assets (USD trillions)

Fifth, policymakers have also responded to recent shocks with huge fiscal

transfers, meaning that debt has migrated from private towards sovereign
balance sheets.
Whilst global debt has risen slightly over the last decade, if you dig deeper into the
numbers, that’s entirely driven by a rise in government debt. If we compare the
statistics for Q2 2026, relative to Q2 2016, there’s actually been a decline in debt for
households, financial corporations, and nonfinancial corporations. The only one of
the four segments that’s gone up in the last decade is government debt.

In relative terms, the transformation is even bigger if we look back to 2007, before
the Global Financial Crisis kicked off. Back then, government debt was the smallest
of the four categories, but because of the GFC and the pandemic, it’s now become
the biggest of the four categories.
That in turn has led to separate issues, with government bond yields at multi-year
highs, alongside rising net interest spending. So the fiscal space has become
limited. However, public balance sheets don’t face the same kind of system-wide

run that a bank would.

### Page 10

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 10 Deutsche Bank AG

Overall, the post-2008 experience offers three key forward-looking lessons. First,
stronger bank balance sheets and the shift towards non-bank finance suggest

future stress may originate outside banks. As such, historical analyses centred on
banking crises may provide a less complete guide. Second, policymakers have
become increasingly effective at containing liquidity pressures and amplification.
And third, the shift in debt towards sovereign balance sheets has cushioned private-
sector shocks but narrowed the capacity for future intervention.
Section 4: What are potential candidates for the next crisis?

This section considers some of the current risks in the financial system and whether
they could be sowing the seeds for the next financial crisis. This section is not
designed to be a comprehensive list of financial risks, but rather an assessment of
three important areas where risks have risen in recent years.
1. Could AI spark broader vulnerabilities?
A huge driver of the current equity bull run has been the growth in AI-related stocks.
This report won’t explore the debate as to whether this is a bubble, but examine

whether a correction could eventually propagate into a systemic financial crisis. As
noted earlier, a reversal in the investment cycle need not cause a crisis in itself, and
the bursting of the dot com bubble also did not lead to a broader financial crisis.
Nevertheless, there are issues that could spark vulnerabilities. First, the rapid rise
in AI capex raises questions about whether the investment will pay off. The AI build-
out is on course to exceed the scale of past technological investment cycles, which

have often involved overinvestment even when the technologies ultimately proved
transformative. Second, funding is shifting from predominantly operating cash
flow towards external capital, and rising debt issuance in particular. Third, greater
equity-market concentration increases exposure to a reassessment of technology
valuations. Together, these developments make a correction a clear risk.
To assess the potential for systemic stress, we apply the framework in Greenwood,

Hanson, Shleifer and Sørensen (GHSS). This associates heightened crisis risk with
the combination of a strong run-up in asset prices and excessive credit growth,
estimating a 45% probability of a crisis within 3 years if the two thresholds in Figure
11 are met. On a backward-looking basis as of 2025, an economy-wide assessment
finds that the equity-price increase meets the framework’s “red zone” test, but
business credit growth has been considerably more subdued, given the slowdown

### Page 11

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Deutsche Bank AG Page 11
in credit following the 2022-23 tightening cycle. That is, going into 2026, the pattern
was one of a displacement of credit from other interest-sensitive sectors towards
AI and technology rather than any broad-based rise in leverage.
Nevertheless, US data does show a clear acceleration in credit trends so far in 2026.
To make a more forward-looking assessment, we incorporate the rise in issuance

in 2026 and indicatively extend it into 2027 using the estimated rise in AI capex
plans, assuming credit growth elsewhere in the US economy remains stable
(footnote: We see two-sided risks to this – we may understate the broader credit
demand in tech adjacent areas; conversely, this may understate the drag of higher
rates on credit demand in non-AI related sectors). We also extend the estimated
2027 trend into 2028, including an assumption that equity prices continue their

trend over the past 12 months, to provide an illustrative scenario over the next two
years. Under these assumptions, we would see a visible rise in aggregate US
business sector leverage, leaving the sector just shy of economy-wide red-zone
levels by 2028. As such, approaching that territory by 2028 is conceivable if the AI
cycle continues to accelerate, both in terms of increased debt borrowing and a
renewed rise in valuations.
While the GHSS framework gives a useful quantification of the potential risks, its

indicators also warrant some qualification, which we judge to come in opposing
directions:
n On the one hand, there is a risk that aggregate credit measures may
understate vulnerabilities when borrowing is highly concentrated. Circular
financing, leases and off-balance-sheet structures complicate the picture,
and while we incorporate the recent rise in payables in our leverage metric
to capture some of this risk, it may well be underestimated. Moreover,

realised interest costs remain below current market borrowing rates after
the lengthy period of ultra-low rates, so leverage trends alone may miss the
pressure from rising debt-service costs.
n On the other hand, the price-based metric used by GHSS to assess excess
asset price growth (the 3-year real rise in equity prices) may overstate
valuation excesses given the strong rise in earnings. Indeed, P/E multiples
have already seen a sizeable correction since late 2025, which was most

pronounced for software stocks, and currently there is little evidence of
overly stretched tech valuations, especially when compared to the dot com
boom.
The risk of stress amplification is also likely to depend on the nature of any AI
correction. A downgrading of the AI capex cycle due to slower growth in AI
demand, financing constraints or supply bottlenecks may result in weaker
valuations, leading to negative wealth effects and slower capital expenditure.

However, while this would create an economic headwind, it need not produce a
systemic crisis. And while parallels to the dot com bubble may well be relevant,
currently there are factors that suggest downside risks would be more moderate.
For instance, operating cash flow still plays an important role in financing the AI
buildout, whilst there has been a limited run-up in borrowing and much less
stretched valuations relative to the late-1990s.
Our assessment therefore remains that an AI correction is a clearer risk than an AI-

led systemic crisis at this point. For these risks to become more material, it would
need leverage to keep building but AI returns to eventually disappoint, raising the
likelihood of credit events and amplifying the investment slowdown through the
real economy. We will focus on this interaction between debt-service pressures and

2. Risks from higher rates and private credit
In the previous section we noted that modest economy-wide debt growth doesn’t
necessarily imply modest credit risk when debt-service costs are rising. As existing
debt reprices or is refinanced, borrowers can face greater pressure even without
additional borrowing. This is a key feature of the debt cycle, whereby vulnerabilities
accumulate during the expansion, and rising rates (often amid a monetary

tightening due to an overheating economy) can then expose them. These risks are
relevant in the context of the current rate hiking cycle, especially as much of the
catch up in debt service costs is yet to play out (see chart below).
With banking-sector risks having moderated since the GFC, the rapid expansion of
private credit and its opaque structure have understandably drawn attention as a
possible source of the next crisis. Parallels with the subprime credit boom have

been periodically made. However, we think these should not be overstated. Even
though the dollar size of the US private credit market is similar to that of subprime
around 2006-07, the market must be assessed relative to the economy (which has
doubled in nominal terms). Moreover, the slower build-up, restrictions on
redemptions and lower risks of direct wealth effects all suggest much more
moderate amplification risks than in 2008.
There are already signs that the private-credit growth cycle is slowing (see chart)

and our credit strategists have noted warning signs including weaker new inflows
into BDCs, increasing payment-in-kind interest and prospective refinancing
pressures. That said, defaults remain limited thus far so these should not be seen
as evidence of an imminent systemic event.

### Page 14

3 November 2026
Thematic Research
Page 14 Deutsche Bank AG
Whether more systemic risks could emerge will depend on issues of solvency,
opacity and capacity for intervention.
n First, while redemption gates can limit immediate cash outflows and
reduce the need to sell illiquid loans under pressure, they do not restore a
borrower’s ability to service debt or absorb underlying credit losses. This
distinction between liquidity protection and solvency is therefore central to

judging whether a slowing credit cycle remains orderly.
n Opacity remains a concern, both in financing structures and in the
distribution of exposures among end-holders, including insurers and other
non-banks. This can make losses harder to identify and potential
amplification harder to assess.
n Policymakers’ success in containing liquidity pressures may not translate
into an effective response to credit events - liquidity provision alone cannot

repair impaired balance sheets. Extending support to opaque non-bank
exposures raises difficult questions about eligibility, loss allocation and the
threshold for intervention.
Quite aside from private credit, the fact that we are back in a rate hiking cycle from
the major central banks also adds to the current risks. Indeed, higher rates bring
down inflation, in part because they tighten financial conditions. But a
consequence of this is that it often reveals vulnerabilities in the financial system,

and makes a crisis more likely. For instance, it was a contributing factor behind the
2023 regional banking crisis, as banks had growing unrealised losses on their
securities portfolio when rates began to rise again. So even if higher rates don’t
directly cause a crisis, they can often be a key contributor. Indeed, hiking cycles
have regularly coincided with some kind of shock, as the experience of recent
decades demonstrates.

3. Stretched fiscal positions and possible NBFI amplifiers
As discussed earlier, the migration of debt towards sovereign balance sheets has

helped cushion private-sector shocks, but this has come at the cost of higher fiscal
risks. Higher public debt and sovereign yields have limited the room for fiscal
stimulus, while persistent inflation has limited the room for monetary stimulus. This
makes another round of coordinated macroeconomic support harder to deliver.
The issue with high public debt is that it if fiscal sustainability is questioned, that can
raise sovereign borrowing costs, which can amplify existing private-sector

vulnerabilities via a tightening in financial conditions and slower growth.
The financial risk posed by sovereign stress will also depend on the financial
amplification channels. These were clear in the European sovereign crisis with the
bank-sovereign loop, but the changing structure of sovereign markets suggests
that the sovereign–NBFI feedback loop may now warrant increasing attention. This
was visible with the 2022 LDI crisis in the UK, while a more recent concern is the run

up in leveraged hedge fund positions in sovereign bond markets, with hedge funds’
sovereign bond exposures more than doubling since 2022 (see chart below). As
noted also by the IMF, this reflects greater issuance and a rising share of sovereign
debt held by hedge funds.
The lessons from Black Monday, LTCM and the LDI crisis offer reassurance that
intervention can contain market stress originating outside the banking system. But
they are not a guarantee that future liquidity shocks will remain economically

benign. The greater scale and interconnectedness of non-bank finance mean that
we can’t simply rely on historical examples today.
Looking forward, a combination of sovereign repricing, leveraged financial
amplification and a more constrained policy response risks proving harder to
resolve than past episodes.
